#5072. 'A Massive Mistake': Tech, AI and Crypto Founders React to Jensen Huang’s Nvidia Exit

'A Massive Mistake': Tech, AI and Crypto Founders React to Jensen Huang’s Nvidia Exit

Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang is out as CEO after 33 years, and some of the loudest names in crypto and artificial intelligence think the company just made a spectacular mistake.

On Thursday, Nvidia confirmed Huang’s resignation as president, CEO, and board member, and named longtime finance chief Colette Kress interim CEO. The move followed reports of two extramarital affairs, including one involving a businesswoman U.S. intelligence reportedly suspected of links to China, as well as concerns over a reported relationship with an Nvidia employee.

Huang acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in a message to employees on Sunday. Nvidia said in a board statement that it had no reason to believe its secrets or national security were compromised, and its independent investigation remains underway. The allegations about foreign intelligence have not established that Huang knowingly provided information to a foreign government.

But even without any leaks, the scandal was enough for the board to decide to let him go.

The market did not welcome the transition. Nvidia shares fell 14% at Thursday’s opening bell; with more than $500 billion wiped from U.S. stocks in a single minute. The company that was once the most valuable in the world has now fallen to third on that list.

Nvidia was already under pressure before the scandal, and Huang’s departure raised a particular problem for a company built so closely around its founder: Who, exactly, can replace him?

Changpeng “CZ” Zhao, the Binance co-founder and one of crypto’s most influential executives, said “it's a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign.” Bitcoin entrepreneur and former Kraken executive Dan Held agreed, writing, “He shouldn't have resigned,” and argued that Huang’s personal problems should be weighed against the company he built.

It's a mistake for Nvidia to let Jensen resign.

— CZ 🔶 BNB (@cz_binance) November 12, 2026

U.S. intelligence officials reportedly assessed Alice Huang as a Chinese intelligence asset, but Nvidia says it has no reason to believe any secrets were compromised, and Singaporean authorities said they lacked sufficient evidence to establish that she committed a crime. As of today, no law enforcement agency has presented any hard evidence against Alice Huang.

Anthony Pompliano, the Bitcoin investor and commentator, moved the conversation from the boardroom to the technology itself. “Do not get distracted by the headlines,” he wrote. “Open-source AI is essential to the freedom of billions of people in the future.”

Open-source AI is essential to the freedom of billions of people in the future.

— Anthony Pompliano 🌪 (@APompliano) November 12, 2026

Surrounding it all is likely the most talked about conspiracy theory in the open-source AI sphere. The ousting was designed to hurt open-source developments, the speculation goes, since Nvidia is one of the leading voices in the open-source movement, which closed-source companies see as a threat.

For Voorhees, the controversy also prompted a practical question. “Is there a decentralized Hugging Face yet?” he asked, alluding to one of Huang’s stated commitments. In other words, the crypto solution to a potential platform bottleneck is to stop trusting a single platform.

That concern is more than a slogan for a founder whose Venice AI business is built around private access to open models. Voorhees previously told Decrypt that the antidote to excessive control over AI was “open-source decentralization.”

Some developers saw a deeper threat in Huang’s departure: the loss of a powerful corporate champion of downloadable AI models, whose underlying parameters, or weights, can be run outside the companies that built them.

The worry is that an Nvidia without Huang could become less willing to confront firms that keep their most advanced models behind proprietary services.

Jun Song, an open-source AI advocate and hardware entrepreneur, alleged that OpenAI and Anthropic had spent millions lobbying Congress to restrict open weights. He pointed to a “Pace the Frontier” campaign and argued that Huang responded by building the Open Secure AI Alliance, ending his post with, “And today, we lost him.”

OpenAI and Anthropic have spent millions lobbying the US Congress to ban open weights.

When they formed the "Pace the Frontier" group to push for strict limits on open models, Jensen stepped up right away.

He created the Open Secure Alliance to fight back.

Look at the list of… pic.twitter.com/OeasotcLO3

In July, Nvidia joined Microsoft, IBM, Hugging Face, and dozens of other organizations to launch the Open Secure AI Alliance, which works on open AI security tools and standards. Nvidia also backed a letter defending open weights against broad restrictions.

The broader conspiracy claim needs, of course, much more evidence. The Pacing the Frontier initiative is a statement from employees at several AI companies seeking tools to slow dangerous frontier development, not proof of a joint corporate campaign to ban all open-weight models or remove Huang.

OpenAI subsequently signed the pro-open-weight letter, while Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei said explicitly that his company had never advocated such a ban.

Other posts on social media showed how much anxiety the debate had generated. A commenter replied to Song that they were now “counting on Huawei and Deepseek for the very survival of the OSS community.”

Huawei and Deepseek are two major Chinese companies, known for their open-source developments

I can't believe now I am counting on Huawei and Deepseek for the very survival of the OSS community. What a loss...

— Look4Newways (@sflk2026) November 12, 2026

The issue comes with a rather large business backdrop. Nvidia agreed in September to acquire the open-source AI repository Hugging Face for roughly $12.9 billion, subject to regulatory clearance, in a deal expected to close in the first half of 2027.

That arrangement could put both the hardware used to build AI and the largest hub used to distribute open models under one company’s influence—arguably, the most powerful company on earth.

Epic Games founder and CEO Tim Sweeney was among the highest-profile tech executives to condemn the exit. “A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity,” he wrote, praising Huang for keeping innovation moving even as Nvidia grew into an enormous company.

A sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity. Jensen founded and ran one of the few companies to reach a massive scale while driving massive continued innovation and ubiquity, while others fell into the trap of ever-increasing extraction. https://t.co/AkS5XT9HF8

— Tim Sweeney (@TimSweeneyEpic) November 12, 2026

Perplexity AI co-founder and CEO Aravind Srinivas took a more personal approach. “Thank you Jensen Huang for 33 years of bold leadership,” he wrote. He also credited Huang’s generosity toward other founders, including himself, for helping shape the future of American AI.

Peter Steinberger, creator of the open-source OpenClaw agent and now at OpenAI, argued that the decision felt rushed. “If that spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal,” he wrote on X. “Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.”

If that Spy’s goal was to destabilize one of the most successful American companies, they achieved their goal. Ousting Jensen feels rushed and not right.

— Peter Steinberger (@steipete) November 12, 2026

Financial commentator The Kobeissi Letter had urged the board to consult shareholders before any decision, arguing that there was no obvious replacement and citing research on the costs of ousting successful CEOs. After the announcement, the account called the resignation “the most costly mistake of the AI Revolution.”

Former DWS executive and corporate-governance critic Desiree Fixler invoked directors’ fiduciary duty, saying the board should protect shareholders instead of indulging what she called a media frenzy.

In general, most of the reactions were supportive, separating personal affairs from business. The corporate espionage angle has apparently not swayed tech enthusiasts.

This Jensen Huang thing is so ugly. Guy takes a company from zero to absolute pinnacle, through blood, sweat, and tears, a pure founder’s founder, great products, makes so many people rich beyond their imaginations, champions open free local AI, and then the incumbent modern…

— Neil Woodfine (@nwoodfine) November 12, 2026

Not everyone outside that chorus thought the security questions could be set aside. North Dakota Republican Sen. Kevin Cramer had called for Huang’s exit, warning about the risk of blackmail and saying the internal investigation was insufficient. The concern from Washington has been largely uniform: the possibility of compromising pressure against the leader of a company central to U.S. AI infrastructure.

Meanwhile, President Donald Trump has remained supportive of Huang: “He’s definitely suffering,” he told the press. “I think he’ll be in good shape.”

Speculation about Huang’s next job spread just as quickly as criticism of his last one. Responding to an X user asking him to hire Huang, Elon Musk replied, “That would be an honor.”

Nvidia says its board is searching for a permanent CEO from inside and outside the company, with Kress running the business in the meantime. Its next scheduled earnings report is November 17, the first major financial update investors will receive after Huang’s departure.

#5070. Louisiana sues Kalshi, Robinhood and other prediction markets in escalating regulatory fight

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Louisiana Attorney General Liz Murrill said she filed a lawsuit against platforms Kalshi, Robinhood, Polymarket, Forecastex, Crypto.com, Coinbase, Novig, Underdog, Gemini and Prophetx.

Louisiana is the latest state to take on prediction market platforms, joining a growing legal battle between states and the federal government over who has jurisdiction to regulate the industry.

On Thursday, Louisiana Attorney General Liz Murrill said she filed a lawsuit against platforms Kalshi, Robinhood, Polymarket, Forecastex, Crypto.com, Coinbase, Novig, Underdog, Gemini and Prophetx for breaking the law.

"For too long, they have violated our laws with impunity," Murrill said in a post on X. "No longer: I am seeking an immediate restraining order and an injunction to restore the rule of law in Louisiana."

Louisiana is now the 11th state to sue one or more prediction markets following Arkansas, Utah and Connecticut, among others. And platforms have fired back. Following the lawsuit on Thursday, Kalshi filed an emergency temporary restraining order in preparation.

"Based on recent discussions with representatives of the state of Louisiana, Kalshi believes that the State will initiate an action against Kalshi in state court to restrict Kalshi from operating in Louisiana," Kalshi said in its court filing.

Prediction markets became massively popular following the 2024 presidential elections, with some of the biggest platforms now worth billions of dollars. States have generally taken issue specifically with sports betting and say that platforms are operating illegally and are avoiding paying taxes. The issue also brings up concerns among tribal organizations about how the platforms could circumvent agreements.

Meanwhile, platforms have argued that the Commodity Futures Trading Commission should be in charge of regulating the platforms, including sports betting. The CFTC has also sued states to get "exclusive jurisdiction," which could later make its way up to the Supreme Court.

On Thursday, a Robinhood spokesperson asserted that its event contracts are federally regulated by the CFTC in response to Louisiana's lawsuit.

"As we've previously shared, Robinhood's event contracts are federally regulated by the CFTC and offered through Robinhood Derivatives, LLC, a CFTC-registered entity, allowing retail customers to access prediction markets in a safe, compliant, and regulated manner," the spokesperson told The Block.

A Coinbase spokesperson called states' approach fragmented.

"Louisiana's lawsuit, filed while multiple federal appellate courts actively consider the same issues, is another example of the fragmented enforcement Congress sought to prevent when it gave the CFTC exclusive jurisdiction over event contracts," the spokesperson said in an emailed statement.

The Coalition for Prediction Markets, which represents several of the platforms sued on Thursday, said the lawsuit was "troubling and unlawful."

"Louisiana’s attack on this industry is a troubling and unlawful attempt to assert state authority over markets governed by federal law that would only cut off people’s access to an important tool for gathering market information, expressing informed views, and hedging risks based on their own expertise," that spokesperson said. "That cannot stand."

Separately, CNBC reported on Wednesday that the CFTC is investigating whether Kalshi failed to inform the CFTC properly about its proposed event contracts.  This comes soon after Reuters reported that the CFTC is investigating Polymarket over whether it violated a deal made in 2022 to promise to ban U.S. users from its flagship overseas platform.

Updated at 10:10 p.m. UTC to include comments

#5069. Illinois Crypto Tax Draws Unlikely Alliance of DOJ, Banks and DeFi

Illinois Crypto Tax Draws Unlikely Alliance of DOJ, Banks and DeFi

An unusually broad coalition, spanning the U.S. Justice Department, Wall Street’s biggest lobby, the national Chamber of Commerce and a who’s-who of crypto firms, has thrown its weight behind a legal challenge to Illinois’ first-of-its-kind tax on digital asset transactions.

The groups filed briefs this month in Illinois state court supporting a lawsuit from the Crypto Council for Innovation and the Blockchain Association, which are asking a judge to strike down the state’s Digital Asset Tax Act and permanently block its enforcement.

The tax imposes a 0.2% levy on the full value of a digital asset every time an Illinois customer trades, transfers, or stores it through a broker, even when there’s no sale, gain, or change in ownership.

Critics have called it among the most punitive crypto taxes in the country, in part because Illinois imposes no comparable tax on dollars, gold, stocks or any other asset, and no other state has anything like it.

On Nov. 6, the Justice Department filed a statement of interest arguing the tax undermines federal policy and U.S. leadership in digital assets, calling it vague and discriminatory for singling out crypto for treatment no other asset faces.

The U.S. Chamber of Commerce and the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association, which represents hundreds of broker-dealers, banks and asset managers, filed an amicus brief warning the tax reaches well beyond crypto-native platforms to the traditional banks, custodians and advisers increasingly offering digital asset products.

A separate brief filed Nov. 11 brought together crypto’s heavy hitters, led by the DeFi Education Fund, Andreessen Horowitz, Paradigm and Uniswap, and including Aave Labs, Chainlink Labs, Solana Policy Institute, Phantom, Jito Labs, Multicoin Capital and Stand With Crypto.

Their filing argues the law’s definitions are so vague they could stretch to software developers and decentralized protocols that hold no customer funds and have no way to identify users or know where a transaction occurred. The brief noted an estimated 2.64 million Illinoisans hold digital assets, the fifth-highest total of any state.

“This tax is unclear, discriminatory, unconstitutional, and harmful to the future of finance,” CCI CEO Ji Hun Kim and Blockchain Association executive Ashok Pinto said in a joint statement.

The challenge dates to August. As Decrypt reported, Illinois agreed in October to pause the tax until July 1, 2027, buying time for the court fight now underway.

#5065. Ethlabs drops Hegotá slot proposal in push for 2027 quantum readiness

Ethlabs’ roadmap aims to help Ethereum withstand quantum attacks and give users a way to protect their accounts by 2027.

Ethereum research group Ethlabs said Wednesday it will withdraw its proposal to speed up the network’s block production from the planned Hegotá upgrade, urging core developers to prepare the network for quantum attacks by 2027.

On Wednesday, the group outlined “pqai27,” a proposed roadmap that aims to protect Ethereum against threats from quantum computing and increasingly capable artificial intelligence. It said unrelated upgrades should be reconsidered.

Ethlabs previously backed Ethereum Improvement Proposal (EIP)-8198, known as Quick Slots, which would initially cut Ethereum’s slot time from 12 seconds to 10 seconds. The group said it would redirect resources to pqai27 but will still pursue faster slots in 2028.

For Hegotá, Ethlabs said FOCIL, a planned censorship-resistance feature, should proceed only if it does not jeopardize quantum readiness. The group called Frame Transactions, which would enable more flexible account authentication, central to the proposed transition.

It said Glamsterdam, the upgrade before Hegotá, should proceed as planned.

Ethereum core developers have not approved the proposal. The Ethereum Foundation’s published roadmap still targets December 2029 for quantum resistance. At Devcon, researcher Justin Drake said he hopes to complete the transition in 2028.

#5064. Gemini trading volume drops 73% as Q3 revenue falls to $41 million

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Gemini narrowed its quarterly loss to $58.6 million as costs fell, while prediction market revenue rose 52% from Q2.

Gemini's trading volume is down 73% from a year earlier in the third quarter, dragging revenue lower despite cost cuts narrowing its losses.

The exchange reported $40.9 million in total revenue on Thursday, down 19% from $50.6 million seen a year ago and 10% from the second quarter. Its net loss shrank to $58.6 million from nearly $160 million a year earlier and $107.7 million in Q2.

Shares of Gemini Space Station Inc. Gemini Space Station Inc. (GEMI) rose 6.5% in after-hours trading to $2.79 but remain down around 75% year to date.

Operating expenses fell 30% year over year to roughly $120 million.

"Operating expenses declined for a third consecutive quarter, and as we invest in growth, we intend to stay disciplined about where those dollars go," Gemini CEO Tyler Winklevoss said in a statement.

Exchange revenue also dropped 47% to $13.4 million, stemming from a trading volume decline to $4.5 billion from $16.4 billion last year at the height of the 2025 crypto market bull run. Still, volume grew 18% from the previous quarter.

Credit card revenue slipped 4% from the prior quarter, bringing in $15.5 million. Meanwhile, assets on the platform jumped to $13.4 billion from Q2's $8.4 billion.

Gemini's prediction market segment generated $800,000 in revenue, up 52% quarter over quarter. The company is looking to make its exchange and clearinghouse open to brokerages that want to offer event contracts.

In August, Gemini signed a letter of intent with Apex Fintech Solutions to offer crypto event contracts through Apex, though the deal is still not finalized.

"Gemini 2.0 is our bet that the platforms that last will be the ones that own the regulated infrastructure underneath," Gemini President Cameron Winklevoss said.

#5063. Nvidia, AMD, Broadcom: The AI Stock Winners and Losers After Jensen Huang’s Ousting

Nvidia, AMD, Broadcom: The AI Stock Winners and Losers After Jensen Huang’s Ousting

Wall Street is picking winners and losers from Jensen Huang's departure from Nvidia, and the market is treating the chipmaker's competitors considerably better than companies tied to its business.

Nvidia shares fell 4.59% to $165.75 Thursday afternoon, recovering from an opening price of $151.01 and an intraday low of $148.80.

The rebound softened an initial collapse of more than 14%, but the stock remained firmly in negative territory.

The selloff followed Huang's immediate resignation as CEO, president, and board member after reports of extramarital affairs and allegations involving a suspected Chinese intelligence operative. Nvidia named CFO Colette Kress interim CEO, although the company has not established that corporate secrets were compromised.

The stock was already in trouble before Huang stepped down. Nvidia had lost approximately $1.27 trillion in market capitalization between October 6 and November 6, raising questions about AI spending even before the leadership crisis intensified.

AMD climbed 5.79% to $584.89 after opening at $569.28, making it one of the clearest beneficiaries of Thursday's trading.

The company competes directly with Nvidia in AI accelerators, and uncertainty around Nvidia's leadership could make customers more interested in diversifying their chip suppliers.

Broadcom followed a similar trajectory, gaining 5.65% to $364.73 from an opening price of $356.80.

Unlike AMD, which competes through its own GPU products, Broadcom specializes in custom AI processors and networking technology, offering large customers a different way to reduce their dependence on Nvidia.

Google’s parent company Alphabet also advanced, rising 2.06% to $299.49 after opening at $293.77.

Those alternatives existed before Huang resigned, and Thursday's gains do not establish that Nvidia has lost customers. Investors may simply be pricing in better competitive opportunities if the leadership transition disrupts Nvidia's strategy or customer relationships.

Nvidia-backed CoreWeave suffered a much rougher session, falling 7.49% to $56.22 after opening at $50.91.

Its decline came just one day after the cloud provider surged 24.7% following quarterly revenue growth of 168%, so this may be just as much a correction after an explosive movement as it is a reaction to Huang’s ousting.

In any case, the relationship between the companies helps explain the anxiety among investors. Nvidia invested another $2 billion in CoreWeave in January and has an initial $6.3 billion agreement to purchase unused computing capacity through April 2032, making it an investor, supplier, and customer of the same company.

Any change in Nvidia's investment priorities could therefore affect CoreWeave beyond ordinary chip purchases, although neither company has announced changes to those agreements.

The pressure also reached the semiconductor supply chain. TSMC, which manufactures Nvidia's advanced processors, fell 1.15% to $408.61 after opening at $394.62, while memory supplier Micron lost 2.70%, trading at $789.43 after opening at $769.31.

SK Hynix, another major supplier of high-bandwidth memory used in AI hardware, declined 3% to $137.98 from an opening price of $134.25.

These companies depend on broader chip demand, however, so their losses cannot be attributed entirely to Nvidia's leadership problems.

For investors, the immediate concern is less about whether AI chips will stop selling and more about how Nvidia manages its partnerships, financing commitments, and product roadmap without the executive who built much of that network.

Nvidia's board has said it expects operations and customer commitments to continue without interruption. The company is scheduled to report quarterly earnings on November 17, with Kress now leading the business through its first earnings announcement since Huang's departure.

The views and opinions expressed by the author are for informational purposes only and do not constitute financial, investment, or other advice.

#5060. Tron and Tether bring in Chainalysis after T3 crime unit folds

The group will focus on illicit USDT activity on Tron and help law enforcement investigate suspicious transfers and freeze funds faster.

Tron, Tether and Chainalysis announced a task force Thursday to identify and disrupt illicit activity involving USDT (USDT) stablecoin on the Tron blockchain.

The Digital Crime Task Force will use blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis’ data and AI tools to flag suspicious activity and give law enforcement leads to trace and freeze funds, according to an announcement shared with Cointelegraph.

Bloomberg reported on Nov. 4 that the T3 Financial Crime Unit, which Tron and Tether formed with TRM Labs in 2024, disbanded in late August. An unnamed source cited by the outlet said slow responses and poor coordination hampered the unit.

Cointelegraph contacted Tether, Tron and TRM Labs for comment but did not receive responses by publication time.

Related: Binance CSO unwittingly foreshadowed $6.3B hack tactic months before attack

The task force’s priorities include investment and romance scams, crypto theft, terrorist financing, North Korean cyber operations and cartel money laundering.

“Criminals are using AI to move faster than ever, and waiting for a victim to report a crime is no longer good enough,” Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said in the announcement. He put Tether’s cumulative USDT freezes at more than $7 billion.

USDT on Tron accounted for 99.9% of the $1.07 billion in crypto received by 34 casinos in Sihanoukville, a Cambodian city known as a hub for scam compounds, from Jan. 1 to Sept. 7, TRM Labs reported.

Casinos tied to US-sanctioned networks received $687 million of those inflows. TRM Labs cited low fees and deep liquidity as reasons for the widespread use of USDT on Tron in the region.

Following the $1.5 billion Bybit hack in February 2025, stolen funds were converted to USDT on Tron and cashed out through suspected Chinese over-the-counter trading services, according to blockchain intelligence company Elliptic.

During the record $6.3 billion Binance hack on Oct. 29, hackers swapped approximately $234 million in USDT for Tron’s native token (TRX) before Tether froze the remaining USDT in two wallets linked to the theft, according to Crystal Intelligence.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation attributed both hacks to North Korea. In a statement published by North Korean state media on Nov. 3, Kim Yo Jong rejected the Binance attribution and cited Bybit as another case in which she said Washington tried to blame Pyongyang.

#5058. DOJ moves to seize $1.2 billion from Binance hack, disrupts North Korean deepfake scheme

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

The DOJ received court approval to seize more than $1.2 billion stolen from Binance and shut down systems used in a separate North Korean scheme involving fake video calls.

The U.S. Department of Justice has obtained court approval to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion stolen from crypto exchange Binance and disrupted a separate North Korean scheme that used fake video calls to steal crypto.

The Binance funds are part of more than $2.7 billion frozen through efforts by U.S. authorities and private companies, the DOJ said Thursday. Investigators are still tracing stolen assets as the hackers move them through crypto bridges, mixers and exchanges, the department said.

The DOJ attributed the Binance hack to North Korean military hackers known as TraderTraitor. The group stole more than $6 billion from the exchange around Oct. 29, according to the department.

"North Korea's government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft," said Principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General for National Security M. Scott Proctor. "The National Security Division will use every tool at its disposal to seize and return North Korea's ill-gotten gains."

Binance previously put its losses at about $6.3 billion and set aside $6.34 billion in company funds to cover them. Earlier Thursday, Binance said it has completed coverage of assets impacted in the hack and published a new proof of reserves snapshot.

Separately, the DOJ said it seized cloud infrastructure used in a North Korean campaign the FBI calls FaceStealer. The scheme has led to more than $400 million in crypto theft over the past 18 months, the department said.

Hackers used stolen accounts to invite victims to video calls, where they played recordings or deepfakes of familiar people. They then faked technical problems and tricked victims into installing malware that could steal crypto or access other systems, the DOJ said.

"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships," said Patrick Grandy, assistant director in charge of the FBI's Los Angeles Field Office. "If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough."

In some cases, the theft and takeover of communication accounts happened months to more than a year after the computer was first compromised, the department said.

Hackers also used stolen contacts and accounts to target more people, including open-source software developers, potentially to carry out attacks through their software. The FBI seized more than 900 webcam videos of FaceStealer victims and issued guidance for those affected, the DOJ said.

#5057. Stablecoin supply falls $14.2B in biggest 30-day drop since June 2022

Crypto markets were left with “less fuel” to rally after the market cap of major stablecoins saw its biggest 30-day drop in over four years.

Stablecoin supply fell by $14.2 billion over 30 days, the biggest drop since June 2022, according to Glassnode.

The rolling 30-day contraction in stablecoin supply hit $14.2 billion this week, more than the $9.5 billion drop seen as Bitcoin (BTC) fell below $58,000 on July 1. It also marks the most pronounced drawdown since June 2022, when supply fell by nearly $30 billion.

Selected stablecoin supply data. Source: Glassnode

Stablecoin liquidity is a key yardstick for gauging crypto market sentiment.

“A shrinking supply means holders are redeeming stablecoins for dollars and taking that cash out of the market, which leaves less fuel for a rally,” Glassnode commented.

USDT, the largest stablecoin by market cap, continues to trade slightly below its $1 peg, per data from TradingView. Glassnode attributes the phenomenon to the “same pressure” from investors rotating out of the stablecoin market.

“Should the 30-day change turn back to growth, it would be the first sign that new money is coming in,” it added.

USDT/USD one-day chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Crypto market metrics continue to point to weakening demand as Bitcoin trades near 11-week lows.

Related: Bitcoin short-term holders send 47K BTC to exchanges at a loss

On Tuesday, onchain analytics platform CryptoQuant that its measure of Bitcoin demand fell by 284,000 BTC over 30 days, the most pronounced downturn since March 2025.

The Coinbase Premium Index, which measures the difference between Coinbase’s BTC/USD price and Binance’s BTC/USDT price, was still negative on Wednesday. The index fell to -0.27% on Oct. 29, its lowest level since November 2022.

“This unusually deep discount suggests weaker relative demand or greater selling pressure on Coinbase, a major venue for US-based trading activity,” CryptoQuant contributing analyst Amr Taha wrote at the time.

Bitcoin Coinbase Premium Index. Source: CryptoQuant

#5055. Who Is Colette Kress, Jensen Huang’s Successor as Nvidia CEO?

Who Is Colette Kress, Jensen Huang’s Successor as Nvidia CEO?

For the first time in 33 years, someone other than Jensen Huang is running Nvidia. And the person taking over is not an engineer, a chip designer, or a Silicon Valley founder.

Colette Kress, Nvidia's 59-year-old chief financial officer, was named interim CEO Thursday following Huang's abrupt resignation amid reports of extramarital affairs and allegations involving a suspected Chinese intelligence operative.

Kress will remain CFO while the board searches for a permanent replacement, according to Nvidia's regulatory filing. She inherits a company worth trillions of dollars, a shaken investor base, and a management structure built around one man.

Kress joined Nvidia in September 2013, when it was primarily known for graphics cards used in video games. She arrived with nearly 25 years of financial experience across some of America's largest technology companies.

She spent eight years at Texas Instruments before joining Microsoft in 1997. During her 13-year tenure there, she became CFO of the Server and Tools division, overseeing financial strategy for Microsoft's enterprise computing business.

In 2010, she moved to Cisco as senior vice president and CFO of its Business Technology and Operations Finance organization, managing financial planning and business development across the networking giant's operations.

Kress holds a finance degree from the University of Arizona and an MBA from Southern Methodist University, according to her official biography.

Her move to Nvidia came with an unusual endorsement. Her two sons, then enthusiastic gamers, encouraged her to join the company responsible for the graphics chips powering their favorite games, according to Forbes.

Huang personally recruited her, explaining his vision for AI computing on a whiteboard during their discussions, Wired reported. At the time, Nvidia was the smallest company Kress had worked for.

When Kress joined Nvidia, its annual revenue stood at approximately $4.13 billion. By fiscal 2026, that figure had reached $215.9 billion, an increase of more than 50 times.

Huang drove Nvidia's technological strategy, but Kress oversaw the finances supporting its expansion from gaming hardware into data centers and AI infrastructure.

Her tenure included Nvidia's $6.9 billion acquisition of networking specialist Mellanox and an increasingly aggressive strategy of investing in companies that purchase its computing hardware.

Those relationships now include CoreWeave, in which Nvidia invested another $2 billion in January. CoreWeave's rapid growth reflects enormous demand for Nvidia-powered computing, alongside the financial costs of building that infrastructure.

Kress also became Nvidia's principal financial voice to Wall Street, regularly explaining margins, chip demand and revenue forecasts during earnings calls.

Forbes ranked her among America's richest self-made women in 2026, with almost $750 million in Nvidia shares as of Wednesday's close.

Kress takes charge as Nvidia faces questions about the sustainability of AI infrastructure spending. Nvidia had already lost approximately $1.27 trillion in market value from its October peak before the board publicly announced its investigation on Huang’s love affairs.

She must also navigate Washington's restrictions on advanced chip exports to China and scrutiny surrounding Nvidia's commercial relationships. The board has said it has no reason to believe company secrets or U.S. national security were compromised in the allegations involving Huang.

Her financial expertise cannot entirely replace Huang's technical background or his personal relationships with developers, customers, and governments. Maintaining Nvidia's engineering leadership will require close coordination with the executives responsible for its chips and software.

Kress has promised to build on Huang's legacy, saying that “Nvidia's strength has always come from its people.”

Her first major public test arrives Tuesday, November 17, when Nvidia reports its third-quarter fiscal 2027 earnings. The company has scheduled its results for approximately 1:20 p.m. Pacific, followed by an investor call at 2 p.m.
